Renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants for the unrolled quantum group of 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 ​ ( ℂ ) \mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Nathan Geer, Adam Robertson, Jan-Luca Spellmann, Matthew B. Young

Abstract
. This paper is a self-contained introduction to the theory of renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links defined by Geer, Patureau-Mirand and Turaev. Whereas the standard Reshetikhin–Turaev theory of a ℂ \mathbb{C} -linear ribbon category assigns the trivial invariant to any link with a component colored by a simple object of vanishing quantum dimension, the renormalized theory does not. We give a streamlined development of the renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev theory of links for the category of weight modules over the restricted unrolled quantum group of 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 2 ​ ( ℂ ) \mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) at an even root of unity.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 81R50; Secondary 57M25.

Introduction

Background and motivation

Let
L\subset S^{3}
be a framed link and
M_{L}
the closed orientable
3
-manifold obtained from
S^{3}
by surgery along
L
. By a theorem of Lickorish and Wallace, any closed connected orientable 3-manifold arises in this way
[
Wal60
,
Lic62
]
. Moreover, the
3
-manifolds
M_{L}
and
M_{L^{\prime}}
are homeomorphic if and only if the framed links
L
and
L^{\prime}
are related by a finite sequence of Kirby moves
[
Kir78
]
. These results are the starting point for a knot theoretic approach to problems and constructions in
3
-manifold topology. For example, it follows from the previous two results that an isotopy invariant of framed links which is also invariant under Kirby moves defines an invariant of
3
-manifolds, thereby emphasizing the topological importance of link invariants.

Reshetikhin and Turaev constructed a large class of link invariants using the theory of ribbon categories
[
RT90
]
. Associated to each ribbon category
\mathcal{D}
is a ribbon functor
F_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
with domain the category of
\mathcal{D}
-colored ribbon graphs. Interpreting an isotopy class of a
\mathcal{D}
-colored framed link
L
as a
(0,0)
-tangle, and so an endomorphism of the unit object
\mathbb{I}\in\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
, produces an invariant
F_{\mathcal{D}}(L)\in\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{I})
of
L
, the
Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant
. The invariant
F_{\mathcal{D}}(L)
is computed as follows. Choose a regular diagram
D
for
L
. Decompose
D
into elementary pieces consisting of cups, caps, simple crossings and twists and assign to these pieces the corresponding coevaluations, evaluations, braidings and twists, respectively, of
\mathcal{D}
. The composition of these morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
is
F_{\mathcal{D}}(L)
.
Figure

1
illustrates this procedure for the Hopf link.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Figure 1.
The Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant of a Hopf link colored by objects
V
and
W
of
\mathcal{D}
. Composition in
\mathcal{D}
is read from bottom to top.

The Reshetikhin–Turaev construction highlights the topological significance of ribbon categories. Classical representation theory produces many examples of symmetric monoidal categories: representations of groups and Lie algebras and, more generally, cocommutative Hopf algebras. Unfortunately, Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants associated to a symmetric monoidal category are uninteresting since they retain information only about the number of components of a link. On the other hand, categories of representations of quantum groups and, more generally, quasi-triangular Hopf algebras famously give rise to (non-symmetric) ribbon categories
[
Jim85
,
Dri86
,
Dri90
,
CP94
]
. The resulting quantum invariants of links, which include the Jones and HOMFLYPT polynomials, are at the foundation of quantum topology
[
Jon87
,
FYH+85
,
PT88
,
RT90
,
Tur16
]
.

Many ribbon categories arising in representation theory have the following properties:

(P1)

The category has simple objects with vanishing quantum dimension.

(P2)

The category is non-semisimple, that is, not every short exact sequence splits.

(P3)

The category has infinitely many non-isomorphic simple objects.

For example, the category
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}){\operatorname{-mod}}
of finite dimensional representations of the quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
associated to a complex simple Lie (super)algebra
\mathfrak{g}
at a root of unity has Properties
(P1)
-
(P3)
. It is well-known that the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant of a link colored by a simple object of vanishing quantum dimension is zero. For this reason, the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction is not well-suited to extracting the full topological content of categories having Property
(P1)
. Properties
(P2)
and
(P3)
do not cause problems for Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links but are serious obstructions to extending these invariants to
3
-manifolds. For example, these properties obstruct the definition of the Kirby color, a weighted sum of isomorphism classes of simple objects, which is crucial to the construction of
3
-manifold invariants in
[
RT91
]
.

A standard approach to simultaneously eliminating Properties
(P1)
-
(P3)
for the category
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}){\operatorname{-mod}}
, with
\mathfrak{g}
a simple Lie algebra, is semisimplification
[
And92
]
, whereby simple objects of vanishing quantum dimension are formally set to zero. The semisimpflied categories are, for particular roots of unity, modular tensor categories. The resulting
3
-manifold invariants comprise the top level of a three dimensional topological quantum field theory which is a mathematical model for Chern–Simons theory with gauge group the simply connected compact Lie group associated to
\mathfrak{g}

[
Wit89
,
RT91
]
. On the other hand, for the category
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}){\operatorname{-mod}}
, with
\mathfrak{g}
a type I Lie superalgebra, typical representations have vanishing quantum dimension and semisimplification eliminates most interesting content of the category.

Ribbon categories with Properties
(P1)
-
(P3)
also arise in quantum field theory. For example, such categories arise as line operators in Chern–Simons theories with non-compact gauge groups
[
Wit91
,
BNW91
,
RS94
,
Mik15
]
and topological twists of supersymmetric quantum field theories
[
KS09
,
CDGG21
]
and as modules for vertex operator algebras in non-rational (or logarithmic) conformal field theories
[
RS93
,
CR13a
,
CR13b
]
.

Early examples of knot invariants constructed from ribbon categories with Properties
(P1)
-
(P3)
include the work of Akutsu, Deguchi and Ohtsuki
[
ADO92
]
and Murakami and Murakami
[
MM01
]
, who defined (framed) link invariants from typical representations of the unrolled quantum group
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
at an even root of unity. A systematic program to define and study quantum invariants from ribbon categories with Properties
(P1)
-
(P3)
, called
renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev theory
, was developed by Blanchet, Costantino, Geer, Patureau-Mirand and Turaev
[
BCGPM16
,
GPMT09
,
CGPM14
]
. In the setting of links, these renormalized invariants provide non-trivial invariants of links colored by objects with vanishing quantum dimension. The goal of this paper is to give a self-contained introduction to the theory of renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links in the simplest case of the category of modules over
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, following
[
GPMT09
,
CGPM15b
]
. While this paper contains no new results, we do offer a number of new proofs of known results and give complete details where they are often not available in the literature. Some familiarity with the representation theory of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, at the level of
[
Jan96
]
, and its associated Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants would be beneficial, but is not strictly necessary. We assume basic knowledge of Hopf algebras and monoidal categories.

Contents of this paper

Fix an integer
r\geq 2
and set
q=e^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}}
. The De Concini–Kac quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
has generators
K^{\pm 1}
,
E
and
F
with relations
KK^{-1}=1=K^{-1}K
and

(1)

KE=q^{2}EK,\qquad KF=q^{-2}FK,\qquad EF-FE=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}.

The
unrolled quantum group

U_{q}^{H}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, as introduced in
[
GPMT09
]
, is defined similarly to the De Concini–Kac quantum group but with an additional generator
H
, thought of as a logarithm of
K
, which commutes with
K
and satisfies the classical limit of the first two relations (
1
):

[H,E]=2E,\qquad[H,F]=-2F.

The algebra of primary interest in this paper is the
restricted unrolled quantum group

\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, defined to be the quotient of
U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
by the relations
E^{r}=F^{r}=0
. A
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-module
V
is called a
weight module
if it is a direct sum of
H
-eigenspaces and
K=q^{H}
as operators on
V
. The category
\mathcal{C}
of finite dimensional weight modules over
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
is the central algebraic object of this paper.

Section

1
is devoted to a detailed study of
\mathcal{C}
. A natural Hopf algebra structure on
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
gives
\mathcal{C}
the structure of a rigid monoidal abelian category. We use Verma modules, which are finite dimensional due to the relations
E^{r}=F^{r}=0
, to classify simple objects of
\mathcal{C}
in
Proposition

1.7
. The result is that there is a discrete family of simple modules
S^{lr}_{n}
of highest weight
n+lr
and dimension
n+1
,
l\in\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq n\leq r-2
, and a continuous family of simple Verma modules
V_{\alpha}
of highest weight
\alpha+r-1
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, and dimension
r
.

Tracking
H
-weights modulo
2\mathbb{Z}
defines a
\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
-grading
\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
which is compatible with the rigid monoidal structure. While the category
\mathcal{C}
is not semisimple, it is
generically semisimple
in the sense that most homogeneous subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}\subset\mathcal{C}
are semisimple. More precisely, we prove in
Theorem

1.13
that
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is semisimple unless
\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
.
Proposition

1.17
states that
\mathcal{C}
is braided. A complete proof of this statement does not seem to be in the literature. The proof we present is elementary and self-contained. The form of the braiding is motivated by the well-known universal
R
-matrix of the
\hbar
-adic quantum group of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

[
Dri86
,
Oht02
]
. In
Theorem

1.19
we prove that
\mathcal{C}
is ribbon. The candidate ribbon structure is based on the twist associated to the rigid monoidal structure, namely the right partial trace of the braiding. We use generic semisimplicity of
\mathcal{C}
to prove that this twist is compatible with duality by checking that this is so generically and concluding, via a general result of
[
GPM18
]
, that this extends to the entirety of
\mathcal{C}
. The results of
Section

1
can be summarized as follows.

Theorem A
.

The category
\mathcal{C}
is a
\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded generically semisimple ribbon category.

In
Section

2
we recall standard material related to the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor
F_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
associated to a ribbon category
\mathcal{D}
. Central to the renormalized theory is the well-known statement, proved in this paper as
Lemma

2.4
, that if
V\in\mathcal{D}
is a simple object of a
\mathbb{C}
-linear ribbon category,
L
is a
\mathcal{D}
-colored link and
T
is a
(1,1)
-tangle with closure
L
and open strand colored by
V
, then

F_{\mathcal{D}}(L)=\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{D}}(V)F_{\mathcal{D}}(T).

Here both sides of the equation are identified with the scalar by which they act. In particular, if
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{D}}(V)=0
, then
F_{\mathcal{D}}(L)
vanishes, while
F_{\mathcal{D}}(T)
need not. We prove in
Proposition

2.6
that it, if
L
is a knot, then
F_{\mathcal{D}}(T)
is an invariant of
L
.

In
Section

3
we extend the invariant
L\mapsto F_{\mathcal{D}}(T)
from framed knots to framed links. To clarify the exposition, we restrict attention to
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}
, the category of weight
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-modules. The main obstacle in this extension is that cutting a link
L
with multiple components produces a
(1,1
)-tangle whose isotopy type depends on the component which is cut. Ambidextrous modules are the key to overcoming this obstacle. A simple
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-module
V
is called
ambidextrous
if the equality

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.76968pt}{-1.98611pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.3658pt}{-23.15257pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\in\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha})\simeq\mathbb{C}.

For a fixed ambidextrous module
V_{\eta}
, the
modified quantum dimension
of a simple Verma module
V_{\alpha}
is defined to be
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)=\frac{S^{\prime}(\alpha,\eta)}{S^{\prime}(\eta,\alpha)}
. The main result of this paper can be stated as follows.

Theorem B
.

(
Theorem

3.12
)

Let
V_{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}
be an ambidextrous module and
L
a framed link with at least one strand colored by
V_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
. Then the assignment

L\mapsto F_{\eta}^{\prime}(L):=\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)F_{\mathcal{C}}(T),

where
T
is a
(1,1)
-tangle whose closure is
L
and whose open strand is colored by
V_{\alpha}
, is a well-defined isotopy invariant of framed colored links.

In
Section

3.4
we discuss some basic properties of the renormalized invariant
F^{\prime}_{\eta}
, such as its behavior under connect sum and its associated skein relations, and compute some basic examples. We also show that renormalizations with respect to different ambidextrous modules
V_{\eta}
lead to invariants which differ by a global scalar.

Finally, in
Section

4
we present a brief guide to further mathematical and physical applications of renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links.

Conventions

The ground field is
\mathbb{C}
. Write
\otimes
for
\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}
. All modules are left modules and finite dimensional over
\mathbb{C}
. Any categorical notion regarding monoidal categories is in congruence with
[
EGNO15
]
. Given a scalar endomorphism
e
of a vector space
V
, define
\langle e\rangle\in\mathbb{C}
by
e=\langle e\rangle\cdot\mathrm{id}_{V}
.

1.
The unrolled quantum group
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
and its weight modules

Fix an integer
r\geq 2
. Set
q=e^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{r}}
.
For
z\in\mathbb{C}
, define

q^{z}=e^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}z}{r}},\qquad\{z\}=q^{z}-q^{-z},\qquad[z]=\frac{\{z\}}{\{1\}}.

Set
\{0\}!=1
and
\{n\}!=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\{i\}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and similarly for
[n]!
. For
0\leq k\leq l
, set
\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{l}{k}=\frac{[l]!}{[k]![l-k]!}
.

1.1.
The unrolled quantum group of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

We recall the definition of the unrolled quantum group of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, as introduced in
[
GPMT09
,
CGPM15b
]
. Pre-cursors of the unrolled quantum group appear in work of Ohtuski
[
Oht02
]
.

Definition 1.1
.

The
unrolled quantum group of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is the unital associative algebra
U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
generated by
K
,
K^{-1}
,
H
,
E
and
F
with relations

KK^{-1}=K^{-1}K=1,\qquad HK=KH,

HE-EH=2E,\qquad HF-FH=-2F,

KE=q^{2}EK,\qquad KF=q^{-2}FK,

EF-FE=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}}.

The
restricted unrolled quantum group

\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
is the quotient of
U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
by the relations
E^{r}=F^{r}=0
.
∎

Informally, the generator
H
should be viewed as a logarithm of
K
. While this constraint is not imposed at the level of algebras, it is imposed on the modules of interest in this paper. See
Section

1.2
below.

Both
U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
and
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
are Hopf algebras with coproduct, counit and antipode defined by

\Delta(E)=1\otimes E+E\otimes K
,

\varepsilon(E)=0
,

S(E)=-EK^{-1}
,

\Delta(F)=F\otimes 1+K^{-1}\otimes F
,

\varepsilon(F)=0
,

S(F)=-KF
,

\Delta(K)=K\otimes K
,

\varepsilon(K)=1
,

S(K)=K^{-1}
,

\Delta(H)=H\otimes 1+1\otimes H
,

\varepsilon(H)=0
,

S(H)=-H
.

The De Concini–Kac quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
is isomorphic to the Hopf subalgebra of
U^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
generated by
E,F
and
K^{\pm 1}
. Similarly, the restricted quantum group
\overline{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
is isomorphic to the Hopf subalgebra of
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
generated by
E
,
F
and
K^{\pm 1}
. The algebra
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
shares many properties with
\overline{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
. For example,
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
has a Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt basis

\{F^{a}H^{b}K^{c}E^{d}\mid 0\leq a,d\leq r-1,\;b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\;c\in\mathbb{Z}\}

and admits a triangular decomposition

\overline{U}^{H,-}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\otimes\overline{U}^{H,0}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\otimes\overline{U}^{H,+}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))

where
\overline{U}^{H,-}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
,
\overline{U}^{H,0}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
and
\overline{U}^{H,+}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
are the subalgebras of
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
generated by
F
,
H
and
K^{\pm 1}
and
E
, respectively. For later use, let
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{b})
be the Hopf subalgebra of
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
generated by
E
,
K^{\pm 1}
and
H
.

1.2.
Weight modules

Recall that all modules are assumed to be finite dimensional.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
V
be a
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-module.

(1)

A
weight vector of weight
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is a non-zero vector
v\in V
which satisfies
Hv=\lambda v
. If, moreover,
Ev=0
, then
v
is called a
highest weight vector
. The subspace
V[\lambda]=\{v\in V\mid Hv=\lambda v\}
is called the
weight space of weight
\lambda
.

(2)

The module
V
is called a
weight module
if it is the direct sum of its weight spaces,
V=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}}V[\lambda]
, and
Kv=q^{\lambda}v
for all
v\in V[\lambda]
.

(3)

The module
V
is called a
highest weight module
if it is generated by a highest weight vector. ∎

All
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-modules considered in this paper are assumed to be weight modules. The second condition in
1.2
(
2
) can be written as the equality as operators
K=q^{H}
on
V
. In view of this, when speaking of weight modules we often give the action of
H
and omit that of
K
. Finally, note that a highest weight module is necessarily a weight module.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of weight
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-modules and their
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-linear maps. The category
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathbb{C}
-linear, locally finite and abelian. The bialgebra structure of
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
makes
\mathcal{C}
into a monoidal category with unit object the one dimensional module
\mathbb{C}
on which
H
,
E
and
F
act by zero. The associators and unitors are as for the category of complex vector spaces and are henceforth suppressed from the notation.

Let
V\in\mathcal{C}
. Denote by
V^{\vee}\in\mathcal{C}
the dual vector space
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V,\mathbb{C})
with
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-module structure given by

(x\cdot f)(v)=f(S(x)v),\qquad x\in\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})),\qquad f\in V^{\vee},\qquad v\in V.

Given a basis
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
V
with dual basis
\{v_{i}^{\vee}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
V^{\vee}
, define

\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}:V\otimes V^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad v\otimes f\mapsto f(K^{1-r}v)

and

\widehat{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow V^{\vee}\otimes V,\qquad 1\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}K^{r-1}v_{i}^{\vee}\otimes v_{i}.

Note that
\widehat{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}
is independent of the choice of basis. A direct check shows that
\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}
are
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-linear and satisfy the snake relations, namely, that the compositions

V\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}}V\otimes V^{\vee}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V}}V

and

(2)

V^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\widehat{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V^{\vee}}}V^{\vee}\otimes V\otimes V^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V^{\vee}}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}}V^{\vee}

are the respective identities. It follows that
\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{coev}}_{V}
are right duality morphisms. Define also

\mathrm{ev}_{V}:V^{\vee}\otimes V\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad f\otimes v\mapsto f(v)

and

\mathrm{coev}_{V}:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow V\otimes V^{\vee},\qquad 1\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}{v_{i}\otimes v_{i}^{\vee}}.

These are the usual left duality morphisms in the category of finite dimensional vector spaces and are easily verified to be
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-linear. It follows that the category
\mathcal{C}
is rigid. Hence,
\mathcal{C}
is tensor in the sense of
[
EGNO15
, Definition 4.1.1]
.

Given a finite dimensional vector space
V
, write
V\rightarrow V^{\vee\vee}
,
v\mapsto\left(\,-\,\middle|\,v\,\right)
, for the canonical evaluation isomorphism.

Lemma 1.3
.

The maps
\{p_{V}:V\rightarrow V^{\vee\vee}\}_{V\in\mathcal{C}}
given by
p_{V}(v)=K^{1-r}\left(\,-\,\middle|\,v\,\right)
define a pivotal structure on
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

We need to verify that
\{p_{V}\}_{V\in\mathcal{C}}
are the components of a monoidal natural isomorphism
p:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow(-)^{\vee}\circ(-)^{\vee}
. Naturality is immediate and a direct check shows that
p_{V}
is
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-linear. The relation
\Delta(K^{1-r})=K^{1-r}\otimes K^{1-r}
implies the equality
p_{V\otimes W}=p_{V}\otimes p_{W}
,
V,W\in\mathcal{C}
, which is the required monoidality.
∎

One can readily see that the right and left duality structures defined above are compatible with the above pivotal structure, in the sense that the equalities
\mathrm{id}_{V^{\vee}}\otimes(p_{V}\circ\widehat{\mathrm{coev}}_{V})=\mathrm{coev}_{V^{\vee}}
and
\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}=(\mathrm{ev}_{V^{\vee}}\circ p_{V})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V^{\vee}}
hold for each
V\in\mathcal{C}
.

1.3.
Simple modules

A non-zero module
V\in\mathcal{C}
is called
simple
(or
irreducible
) if it has no non-zero proper submodules. In this section, we classify simple objects of
\mathcal{C}
. The results of this section are contained in
[
CGPM15b
, §5]
, although we give different proofs.

Lemma 1.4
.

Every simple object of
\mathcal{C}
is a highest weight module.

Proof.

Let
V\in\mathcal{C}
be simple and
v\in V
a weight vector. Since
E^{r}=0
, there exists a minimal integer
l>0
such that
E^{l}v=0
. Then
E^{l-1}v
is a highest weight vector and
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\cdot E^{l-1}v\subset V
is a non-zero submodule which, by simplicity, is equal to
V
.
∎

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
. Denote by
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+r-1}
the one dimensional weight
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{b})
-module of
H
-weight
\alpha+r-1
on which
E
and
F
act by zero.

Definition 1.5
.

The
Verma module of highest weight
\alpha+r-1
is the
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-module

V_{\alpha}=\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\otimes_{\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{b})}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+r-1}.

∎

Write
v_{i}
for the vector
F^{i}\otimes 1\in V_{\alpha}
. The Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt basis for
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
shows that
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}\}
is a weight basis of
V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}
. Direct calculations show that the
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-action on
V_{\alpha}
is given by

Hv_{i}=(\alpha+r-1-2i)v_{i},\quad Ev_{i}=\frac{\{i\}\{i-\alpha\}}{\{1\}^{2}}v_{i-1},\quad Fv_{i}=v_{i+1},

where by convention
v_{-1}=v_{r}=0
. In particular,
V_{\alpha}
is a highest weight module generated by
v_{0}
. The structure of
V_{\alpha}
is summarized by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{r-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{r-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H=\alpha-r+1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H=\alpha-r+3}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H=\alpha+r-3}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H=\alpha+r-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Lemma 1.6
.

If
V
is a highest weight module of highest weight
\alpha+r-1
, then there exists a surjection
V_{\alpha}\twoheadrightarrow V
.

Proof.

By adjunction, there is an isomorphism

\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha},V)\simeq\textnormal{Hom}_{\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{b})}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha+r-1},V_{\big|\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{b})}).

It follows that
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha},V)
is isomorphic to the subspace of highest weight vectors of weight
\alpha+r-1
in
V
. In particular, if
v\in V
is a generating highest weight vector of weight
\alpha+r-1
, then the assignment
v_{0}\mapsto v
extends to a surjective morphism
V_{\alpha}\rightarrow V
in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Using
Lemma

1.6
, it is straightforward to verify that the map
v_{\alpha,r-1}^{\vee}\mapsto v_{-\alpha,0}
extends to a
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-module isomorphism

(3)

V_{\alpha}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\sim}V_{-\alpha}.

It follows from
Lemmas

1.4
and
1.6
that any simple object of
\mathcal{C}
is a quotient of a unique Verma module
V_{\alpha}
. In particular, a simple module has dimension at most
r
.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

If
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}\setminus r\mathbb{Z}
, then
V_{\alpha}
is simple.

(2)

If
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus r\mathbb{Z}
is written in its unique form as
\alpha=(l-1)r+n+1
with
0\leq n\leq r-2
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, then there exists a non-split short exact sequence

0\rightarrow S_{r-n-2}^{(l-1)r}\rightarrow V_{\alpha}\rightarrow S_{n}^{lr}\rightarrow 0

which is a Jordan–Hölder filtration of
V_{\alpha}
.

(3)

Any simple object of
\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to a unique module of the form
V_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, or
S_{n}^{lr}
,
l\in\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq n\leq r-2
.

Proof.

If
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}\setminus r\mathbb{Z}
, then
\frac{\{i\}\{i-\alpha\}}{\{1\}^{2}}\neq 0
for
i=1,\dots,r-1
, as follows from the assumption that
q
is a primitive
2r
th
root of unity. It follows from the explicit form of the action of
E
on
V_{\alpha}
that
Ev_{i}\neq 0
for
i=1,\dots,r-1
, whence
V_{\alpha}
is simple.

If instead
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus r\mathbb{Z}
, then
V_{\alpha}
has exactly one proper submodule. Indeed, write
\alpha=(l-1)r+n+1
as in the statement of the proposition, so that
V_{\alpha}
is of highest weight
lr+n
. Examining the action of
E
on
V_{\alpha}
shows that
Ev_{n+1}=0
and
Ev_{i}\neq 0
if
i\neq 0,n+1
. Hence,
S:=\text{span}\{v_{n+1},\ldots,v_{r-1}\}
is the unique proper submodule of
V_{\alpha}
. The module
S
has dimension
r-n-1
and its quotient
S_{n}^{lr}:=V_{\alpha}/S
is a simple highest weight module of highest weight
lr+n
and dimension
n+1
. By
Lemma

1.6
, there exists a surjection
V_{(l-1)r-n-1}\rightarrow S
which, by the argument of this paragraph, descends to an isomorphism
S_{r-n-2}^{(l-1)r}\xrightarrow{\sim}S
. Finally, the uniqueness of
S
implies that the sequence

0\rightarrow S_{r-n-2}^{(l-1)r}\rightarrow V_{\alpha}\rightarrow S_{n}^{lr}\rightarrow 0

is non-split.

By
Lemmas

1.4
and
1.6
, any simple module is a quotient of a Verma module. Thus, the third statement of the proposition follows from the first two.
∎

Remark 1.8
.

Since
\overline{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
is a Hopf subalgebra of
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, there is a monoidal forgetful functor
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\overline{U}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})){\operatorname{-mod}}
. In the notation of
[
Jan96
, §2.11]
, this functor sends the simple objects
S^{lr}_{n}
and
V_{\alpha}
of
\mathcal{C}
to
L(n,(-1)^{l})
and
Z_{0}(q^{\alpha+r-1})
, respectively.
∎

Proposition

1.7
implies that a simple object is determined up to isomorphism by its highest weight and that the simple objects
S^{lr}_{n}
are neither injective nor projective.

Proposition 1.9
.

If
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, then
V_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}
is projective and injective.

Proof.

Let
f:V\twoheadrightarrow W
be a surjection in
\mathcal{C}
and
\phi:V_{\alpha}\rightarrow W
a non-zero morphism. By the proof of
Lemma

1.6
, the map
\phi
is determined by a highest weight vector
\phi(v_{0})=w\in W
of weight
\alpha+r-1
. Surjectivity of
f
implies that
w
has a preimage under
f
, say
v
, which is of weight
\alpha+r-1
and satisfies
Ev\in\ker f
.

Let
\xi=Ev
, which is of weight
\alpha+r+1
and satisfies
E^{r-1}\xi=0
. For any
a_{0},\dots,a_{r-2}\in\mathbb{C}
, the vector

v^{\prime}=v+\sum_{i=0}^{r-2}a_{i}F^{i+1}E^{i}\xi.

is of weight
\alpha+r-1
and satisfies
f(v^{\prime})=w
. Using
[
Jan96
, §1.3]
, we compute

Ev^{\prime}=\xi+\sum_{i=0}^{r-2}a_{i}(F^{i+1}E^{i+1}\xi+[i+1][\alpha+r+1+i]F^{i}E^{i}\xi).

Then
Ev^{\prime}=0
if and only if the recursive equations

a_{i}=-[i+2][\alpha+r+2+i]a_{i+1}\qquad i=-1,\dots,r-3,

hold, with
a_{-1}=1
. This recursive system determines
\{a_{i}\}_{i}
if and only if
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, as otherwise the coefficient of some
a_{i+1}
vanishes. Arguing as in the start of the proof, the assignment
v_{0}\mapsto v^{\prime}
determines a
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-linear map
\tilde{\phi}:V_{\alpha}\rightarrow V
which satisfies
f\circ\tilde{\phi}=\phi
. This establishes the projectivity of
V_{\alpha}
.

In view of the isomorphism (
3
) and the previous paragraph, the module
V_{\alpha}^{\vee}
is projective. Standard adjunction isomorphisms (see
[
EGNO15
, Proposition 2.10.8]
) give a natural isomorphism of contravariant functors

\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,V_{\alpha})\simeq\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha}^{\vee},-)\circ(-)^{\vee}.

Because
V_{\alpha}^{\vee}
is projective,
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha}^{\vee},-)
is an exact functor. Because
(-)^{\vee}
is an exact functor at the level of complex vector spaces, it is also exact on
\mathcal{C}
. Hence, the functor
\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,V_{\alpha})
is exact and
V_{\alpha}
is injective.
∎

1.4.
Generic semisimplicity

Recall that an abelian category is called
semisimple
if every object is a direct sum of simple objects. In view of
Proposition

1.7
(
2
), the category
\mathcal{C}
is not semisimple. However,
\mathcal{C}
fails to be semisimple in a controlled manner. The goal of this section is to make this statement precise. To do so, we begin with some general definitions from
[
GPM18
]
.

Let
G
be an additive abelian group.

Definition 1.10
.

A
G
-grading
on a rigid monoidal category
\mathcal{D}
is the data of non-empty full subcategories
\mathcal{D}_{g}\subset\mathcal{D}
,
g\in G
, such that
\mathcal{D}=\bigoplus_{g\in G}\mathcal{D}_{g}
and
V^{\vee}\in\mathcal{D}_{-g}
and
V\otimes V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{g+g^{\prime}}
whenever
V\in\mathcal{D}_{g}
and
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{g^{\prime}}
.
∎

Definition 1.11
.

A subset
X\subset G
is called
symmetric
if
-X=X
and
small
if
G\neq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}(g_{i}+X)
for all
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}\in G
.
∎

Definition 1.12
.

A
G
-graded category
\mathcal{D}
is called
generically semisimple with small symmetric subset
X\subset G
if
\mathcal{D}_{g}
is semisimple whenever
g\in G\setminus X
. In this case, a simple module
V\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
in degree
g\in G\setminus X
is called
generic simple
.
∎

Consider again the category of weight modules over
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
. Let
G
be the additive group
\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
. For each
\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of modules whose weights are in the class
\overline{\alpha}
. The Hopf algebra structure of
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
shows that
\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is a
\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
-grading.

Theorem 1.13
.

The
\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded category
\mathcal{C}
is generically semisimple with small symmetric subset
\mathbb{Z}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{C}/\penalty 2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{\alpha}\in(\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})\setminus(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
and
V\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
a non-zero object. Then
V
contains a highest weight vector
v
of weight
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, where
\alpha
is in the class
\overline{\alpha}
. The assumption on
\overline{\alpha}
implies that the submodule generated by
v
is isomorphic to
V_{\alpha-r+1}
; see the proof of
Proposition

1.7
. The module
V_{\alpha-r+1}
is injective by
Proposition

1.9
, whence there is a splitting
V\simeq V_{\alpha-r+1}\oplus V^{\prime}
for some
V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}
of dimension strictly less than that of
V
. An induction argument on the dimension of
V
then completes the proof.
∎

In view of
Proposition

1.7
, the generic simple objects of
\mathcal{C}
are the Verma modules
V_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
.

1.5.
Braiding

In this section, we construct a braiding on
\mathcal{C}
. The form of the braiding is motivated by the universal
R
-matrix for the
\hbar
-adic quantum group
U_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, as described in
[
Dri86
, §10]
,
[
Oht02
, §§4.5 and A.2]
.

Definition 1.14
.

The
r
-truncated q-exponential map
is
\exp_{q}^{<}(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{q^{l(l-1)/2}}{[l]!}x^{l}
.
∎

Let
V,W\in\mathcal{C}
with weight bases
\{v_{i}\}_{i}
and
\{w_{j}\}_{j}
of weights
\{\lambda_{i}^{v}\}_{i}
and
\{\lambda_{j}^{w}\}_{j}
, respectively. Define
q^{H\otimes H/2}\in\textnormal{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V\otimes W)
by

q^{H\otimes H/2}(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})=q^{\lambda_{i}^{v}\lambda_{j}^{w}/2}v_{i}\otimes w_{j}

and
R\in\textnormal{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V\otimes W)
as

R=q^{H\otimes H/2}\circ\exp_{q}^{<}(\{1\}E\otimes F)=q^{H\otimes H/2}\circ\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l}}{\{l\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}E^{l}\otimes F^{l},

where
\exp_{q}^{<}(\{1\}E\otimes F)
is viewed as a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map via left multiplication. Finally, define
c_{V,W}\in\textnormal{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V\otimes W,W\otimes V)
as

c_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=\tau R(v\otimes w),

where
\tau
is the swap map
V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V
,
v\otimes w\mapsto w\otimes v
.

Lemma 1.15
.

The map
c_{V,W}
is
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-linear.

Proof.

It suffices to check linearity of
c_{V,W}
on the generators
H,F,E
. Let
v\in V
and
w\in W
of weight
\lambda^{v}
and
\lambda^{w}
, respectively. We have

\displaystyle H\cdot c_{V,W}(v\otimes w)

\displaystyle=\tau q^{H\otimes H/2}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{q^{l(l-1)/2}}{[l]!}E^{l}\otimes F^{l}((H+2l)v\otimes w+v\otimes(H-2l)w)

\displaystyle=(\lambda^{v}+\lambda^{w})c_{V,W}(v\otimes w)

\displaystyle=c_{V,W}(H\cdot v\otimes w).

We prove
E
-linearity. We have an equality

K\otimes E\circ q^{H\otimes H/2}=q^{H\otimes H/2}\circ 1\otimes E

in
\textnormal{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V\otimes W)
. Indeed, we compute

K\otimes E\circ q^{H\otimes H/2}(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})=q^{\lambda^{v}_{i}}q^{\lambda^{v}_{i}\lambda^{w}_{j}/2}v_{i}\otimes Ew_{j}=q^{\lambda^{v}_{i}(\lambda^{w}_{j}+2)/2}v_{i}\otimes Ew_{j}

and

q^{H\otimes H/2}\circ 1\otimes E(v_{i}\otimes w_{j})=q^{H\otimes H/2}v_{i}\otimes Ew_{j}=q^{\lambda^{v}_{i}(\lambda^{w}_{j}+2)/2}v_{i}\otimes Ew_{j}.

A similar calculation shows that
E\otimes 1\circ q^{H\otimes H/2}=q^{H\otimes H/2}\circ E\otimes K^{-1}
. Using these two equalities,
E
-linearity of
c_{V,W}
reduces to the equality

(E\otimes K^{-1}+1\otimes E)\exp_{q}^{<}(\{1\}E\otimes F)=\exp_{q}^{<}(\{1\}E\otimes F)(E\otimes K+1\otimes E)

in
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\otimes\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, which is proved in
[
Oht02
, Equation A.10]
. Linearity of
F
is proved similarly.
∎

Lemma 1.16
.

The map
c_{V,W}
is invertible.

Proof.

Clearly
\tau
is invertible. We claim that the inverse of
R
is

R^{-1}=\exp_{q^{-1}}^{<}(-\{1\}E\otimes F)q^{-H\otimes H/2}.

Compare with
[
Oht02
, §A.2]
.
We have
q^{H\otimes H/2}\circ q^{-H\otimes H/2}=1
.
By definition,

\exp_{q}^{<}(\{1\}E\otimes F)\cdot\exp_{q^{-1}}^{<}(-\{1\}E\otimes F)\\
=\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\frac{q^{l(l-1)/2}q^{-k(k-1)/2}}{[l]![k]!}(-1)^{k}(\{1\}E\otimes F)^{l+k}.

Since
(E\otimes F)^{r}=0
, the double sum is

\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{q^{-i(i-1)/2}}{[i]!}(-\{1\}E\otimes F)^{i}\sum_{l=0}^{i}(-1)^{l}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{i}{l}q^{l(i-1)}.

The sum
\sum_{l=0}^{i}(-1)^{l}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{i}{l}q^{l(i-1)}
is
0
for
i>0
and
1
if
i=0
; see
[
Jan96
, §0.2]
. The inverse of
R
is thus as stated.
∎

Proposition 1.17
.

The maps
\{c_{V,W}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V\}_{V,W}
define a braiding on
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Lemmas

1.15
and
1.16
yield that the maps
c_{V,W}
give a family of isomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. Naturality of
c_{V,W}
follows from the fact that the endomorphism
q^{H\otimes H/2}
commutes with
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-linear maps.
It remains to verify the hexagon axioms
[
EGNO15
, Definition 8.1.1]
. Let
V,W,U\in\mathcal{C}
. We prove that

(4)

c_{V,W\otimes U}=(\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes c_{V,U})\circ(c_{V,W}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U})

and leave the verification of the equality
c_{V\otimes W,U}=(c_{V,U}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{V}\otimes c_{W,U})
to the reader. Let
v\in V,w\in W,u\in U
be weight vectors of weights
\lambda^{v},\lambda^{w}
,
\lambda^{u}
, respectively.
We compute

c_{V,W\otimes U}(v\otimes w\otimes u)=q^{H\otimes H/2}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l}}{\{l\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}\Delta(F^{l})(w\otimes u)\otimes E^{l}v.

A straightforward induction argument shows that

\Delta(F^{l})=\sum_{i=0}^{l}q^{i(l-i)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{l}{i}F^{i}K^{-(l-i)}\otimes F^{l-i}.

Compare with
[
Jan96
, §3.1]
, where slightly different conventions are used. Using this, we find

c_{V,W\otimes U}(v\otimes w\otimes u)=q^{H\otimes H/2}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l}}{\{l\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}\sum_{i=0}^{l}q^{(i-\lambda^{w})(l-i)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{l}{i}F^{i}\otimes F^{l-i}\otimes E^{l}(w\otimes u\otimes v)

which evaluates to

\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l}}{\{l\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}\sum_{i=0}^{l}q^{(\lambda^{w}+\lambda^{u}-2l)(\lambda^{v}+2l)/2}q^{(i-\lambda^{w})(l-i)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{l}{i}F^{i}\otimes F^{l-i}\otimes E^{l}(w\otimes u\otimes v).

On the other hand, we compute

\displaystyle c_{V,W}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U}(v\otimes w\otimes u)

\displaystyle=q^{H\otimes H/2}\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l}}{\{l\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}F^{n}w\otimes E^{n}v\otimes u

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l}}{\{l\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}q^{(\lambda^{w}-2l)(\lambda^{v}+2l)/2}F^{l}w\otimes E^{l}v\otimes u.

Applying
\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes c_{V,U}
then gives

\mathrm{id}_{W}\otimes c_{V,U}(\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l}}{\{l\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}q^{(\lambda^{w}-2l)(\lambda^{v}+2l)/2}F^{l}w\otimes E^{l}v\otimes u)\\
=\sum_{l=0}^{r-1}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}\frac{\{1\}^{2l+2k}}{\{l\}!\{k\}!}q^{l(l-1)/2}q^{k(k-1)/2}q^{(\lambda^{w}-2l)(\lambda^{v}+2l)/2}\cdot\\
\cdot q^{(\lambda^{u}-2k)(\lambda^{v}+2l+2k)/2}\cdot(F^{l}w\otimes F^{k}u\otimes E^{k}E^{l}v).

To check the equality
c_{V,W\otimes U}(v\otimes w\otimes u)=c_{V,W}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{U}(v\otimes w\otimes u)
, we compare the coefficients of
F^{a}w\otimes F^{b}u\otimes E^{a+b}v
. The coefficients on the left and right-hand sides of the desired equality are

\frac{\{1\}^{2(a+b)}}{\{a+b\}!}q^{(a+b)((a+b)-1)/2}q^{(\lambda^{w}+\lambda^{u}-2(a+b))(\lambda^{v}+2(a+b))/2}q^{(a-\lambda^{w})((a+b)-a)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{a+b}{a}

and

\frac{\{1\}^{2a+2b}}{\{a\}!\{b\}!}q^{a(a-1)/2}q^{b(b-1)/2}q^{(\lambda^{w}-2a)(\lambda^{v}+2a)/2}q^{(\lambda^{u}-2b)(\lambda^{v}+2a+2b)/2},

respectively, which are equal by direct verification. ∎

1.6.
Ribbon structure

In this section, we construct a ribbon structure on
\mathcal{C}
. Having already established that
\mathcal{C}
is braided (
Proposition

1.17
), a ribbon structure is the additional data of a
twist
, that is, a natural automorphism of the identity functor
\theta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
which satisfies the
balancing condition

\theta_{V\otimes W}=(\theta_{V}\otimes\theta_{W})\circ c_{W,V}\circ c_{V,W}

and the
ribbon condition

(5)

(\theta_{V})^{\vee}=\theta_{V^{\vee}}

for all
V,W\in\mathcal{C}

Recall that the right partial trace of
f\in\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V\otimes W)
is the endomorphism
\mathrm{ptr}_{R}(f)\in\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)
defined by

V\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{W}}V\otimes W\otimes W^{\vee}\xrightarrow{f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{W^{\vee}}}V\otimes W\otimes W^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{W}}V.

Define a natural automorphism
\theta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
by

(6)

\theta_{V}:=\mathrm{ptr}_{R}(c_{V,V}),\qquad V\in\mathcal{C}

where
c
is the braiding of
\mathcal{C}
. The hexagon axioms of the braiding ensure that
\theta
satisfies the balancing condition. To verify that
\theta
also satisfies the ribbon condition, we use the following generic extension result.

Theorem 1.18

(
[
GPM18
, Theorem 9]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a generically semisimple pivotal braided category. Define a natural automorphism
\theta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
so that its components are given by Equation (
6
). If
\theta_{V}^{\vee}=\theta_{V^{\vee}}
for any generic simple object
V\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\theta
is a twist on
\mathcal{D}
.

We can now prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 1.19
.

The natural transformations
c
and
\theta
equip
\mathcal{C}
with the structure of a ribbon category.

Proof.

Recall that the generic simple objects of
\mathcal{C}
are the Verma modules
V_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
. For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
the right partial trace of
c_{V_{\alpha},V_{\alpha}}
is

V_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{V_{\alpha}}}V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{c_{V_{\alpha},V_{\alpha}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}^{\vee}}}V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V_{\alpha}}}V_{\alpha}.

Since
\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha})\simeq\mathbb{C}
(see the proof of
Lemma

1.6
), it suffices to compute the image of the highest weight vector
v_{0}\in V_{\alpha}
under this composition. We have

v_{0}\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}v_{0}\otimes v_{i}\otimes v_{i}^{\vee}\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\alpha+r-1-2i)/2}v_{i}\otimes v_{0}\otimes v_{i}^{\vee}\\
\mapsto q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\alpha+r-1)/2}q^{(\alpha+r-1)(1-r)}v_{0}=q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\alpha-r+1)/2}v_{0}.

Since
V^{\vee}_{\alpha}\simeq V_{-\alpha}
and the scalar
q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\alpha-r+1)/2}
is unchanged under the substitution
\alpha\mapsto-\alpha
, it follows that
\theta_{V_{\alpha}^{\vee}}=\theta_{V_{\alpha}}^{\vee}
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
.
Theorem

1.18
therefore applies in the present setting, allowing the conclusion that the maps
\{\theta_{V}\}_{V\in\mathcal{C}}
define a twist on
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

2.
Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants

We recall basic background material on Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links
[
RT90
]
. For a detailed introduction to the theory, the reader is referred to
[
Tur16
]
. We end this section by modifying the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction to produce a non-zero invariant for knots colored by simple objects of vanishing quantum dimension. Readers who are well-versed in Reshetikhin–Turaev theory could remind themselves of Lemma
2.4
and proceed to
Section

3
.

2.1.
Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a ribbon category. Associated to
\mathcal{D}
is the ribbon category of
\mathcal{D}
-colored ribbon graphs
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}

[
Tur16
, §I.I.2]
. Objects of
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
are finite sequences of pairs
(V,\epsilon)
, where
V\in\mathcal{D}
and
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
. Morphisms in
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
are isotopy classes of
\mathcal{D}
-colored ribbon graphs bordering two such sequences of objects. The colorings of the ribbon graphs are required to be compatible with the domain and codomain objects in the obvious sense. Composition of morphisms is defined by concatenation of ribbon graphs. The monoidal structure of
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
is defined on objects by concatenation of sequences and on morphisms by disjoint union.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
Tur16
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

There exists a unique ribbon functor
F_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that
F_{\mathcal{D}}(V,+)=V
and
F_{\mathcal{D}}(V,-)=V^{\vee}
for all
V\in\mathcal{D}
.

The functor
F_{\mathcal{D}}
is called the
Reshetikhin–Turaev functor
. The precise definition of the ribbon structure of
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
and the fact that
F_{\mathcal{D}}
is ribbon implies that
F_{\mathcal{D}}
takes the following values on morphisms in
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
:

F_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\hbox to19.77pt{\vbox to29.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.32156pt\lower-0.59999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

The above eight morphisms in
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
generate all morphisms of
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}

[
Tur16
, §I.3-4]
. In particular, the value
F_{\mathcal{D}}
on any morphism of
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
can be computed as an iterated composition of (co)evaluations, (inverse) braidings and (inverse) twists in
\mathcal{D}
. Colored framed links are particular examples of morphisms in
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{D}}
- they are endomorphisms of the empty sequence. Thus, the assignment
L\mapsto\langle F_{\mathcal{D}}(L)\rangle
is a isotopy invariant of colored framed links.

We record the following result which will be used below.

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
V,W\in\mathcal{D}
, the following equality of morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
holds:

The snake relation (
2
) implies that the second scalar in the final line is
1
.
∎

2.2.
Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants and quantum dimension

Let
K
be the unknot. Color
K
by an object
V
of a
\mathbb{C}
-linear ribbon category
\mathcal{D}
. The scalar
\langle F_{\mathcal{D}}(K)\rangle
associated to the map
F_{\mathcal{D}}(K):\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is called the
quantum dimension of
V
and is denoted by
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{D}}(V)
. Explicitly, we have
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{D}}(V)=\langle\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V}\circ\mathrm{coev}_{V}\rangle
.

Example 2.3
.

Consider again the category
\mathcal{C}
of weight
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-modules. Let
K
be the unknot colored by
V_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
. Let
\{v_{i}\mid 0\leq i\leq r-1\}
be the weight basis of
V_{\alpha}
described in
Section

1.3
with
\{v_{i}^{\vee}\mid 0\leq i\leq r-1\}
its dual basis. Then
F_{\mathcal{C}}(K)
is the composition

1\xmapsto{\mathrm{coev}_{V_{\alpha}}}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}v_{i}\otimes v_{i}^{\vee}\xmapsto{\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V_{\alpha}}}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{(\alpha+r-1-2i)(1-r)}=q^{(\alpha+r-1)(1-r)}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{-2i+2ir}.

As
q
is a primitive
2r
th
root of unity, we have
\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{-2i+2ir}=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{-2i}=0
. Hence,
F_{\mathcal{C}}(K)=0
and
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha})=0
. If instead
K
is colored by the simple module
S^{lr}_{n}
,
0\leq n\leq r-2
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

\langle F_{\mathcal{C}}(K)\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}v_{j}^{\vee}(K^{1-r}v_{j})=q^{(1-r)(lr+n)}\sum_{j=0}^{n}q^{-2j}=(-1)^{n+l+lr}[n+1],

whence
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(S^{lr}_{n})\neq 0
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-linear ribbon category,
V\in\mathcal{D}
a simple object,
L
a
\mathcal{D}
-colored link and
T
a
(1,1)
-tangle whose closure is
L
and whose open strand is colored by
V
. Then

(9)

\langle F_{\mathcal{D}}(L)\rangle=\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{D}}(V)\langle F_{\mathcal{D}}(T)\rangle.

Proof.

Using isotopy invariance we can draw a diagram of
L
of the form

Since
V
is simple, the endomorphism
F_{\mathcal{D}}(T)
is a scalar and
Equation

9
follows.
∎

Thus, whenever a knot is colored by a simple object of vanishing quantum dimension, the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant is trivial. In particular, in view of
2.3
, the Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of
\mathcal{C}
-colored links with at least one component colored by a simple Verma module are zero.

2.3.
Knot invariants via cutting

Equation

9
is the starting point of the theory of renormalized quantum invariants of
[
GPMT09
]
. The main idea is that even though
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{D}}(V)
, and hence
F_{\mathcal{D}}(K)
, vanish,
F_{\mathcal{D}}(T)
need not and may provide an interesting invariant of
K
. In graphical language, to get a non-trivial invariant of a knot
K
we cut it to obtain a
(1,1)
-tangle
T
and apply the standard Reshetikhin–Turaev functor to
T
.

T=\hbox to60.18pt{\vbox to62.37pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.67151pt\lower 3.66795pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Figure 2.
A
(1,1)
-tangle
T
whose closure is the right-handed trefoil.

Example 2.5
.

Let
K
be the right-handed trefoil knot colored by a Verma module
V_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}
.
2.3
shows that
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha})=0
. It follows from
Lemma

2.4
that
F_{\mathcal{C}}(K)=0
.

Let
T
be the
(1,1)
-tangle pictured in
Figure

2
. The closure of
T
is
K
. The endomorphism
F_{\mathcal{C}}(T)\in\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha})
is the composition

V_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{V_{\alpha}}}V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{(c_{V_{\alpha},V_{\alpha}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}^{\vee}})^{\circ 3}}V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\alpha}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V_{\alpha}}}V_{\alpha}.

Since
V_{\alpha}
is highest weight,
F_{\mathcal{C}}(T)
is determined by its value on a highest weight vector
v_{0}\in V_{\alpha}
. Using the explicit form of the braiding, we compute

\langle F_{\mathcal{C}}(T)\rangle=q^{\frac{3}{2}(\alpha+r-1)^{2}+(\alpha+r-1)(1-r)}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{i(-3\alpha-r+1)}\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\{i-j-\alpha\}.

For example, when
r=2
and
\alpha=2
, this specializes to
\langle F_{\mathcal{C}}(T)\rangle=-3e^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{-1}}{4}}\neq 0
.
∎

Proposition 2.6
.

The assignment
K\mapsto\langle F_{\mathcal{D}}(T)\rangle
, where
K
is a colored framed knot and
T
is a
(1,1)
-tangle whose closure is
K
, is a well-defined invariant of colored framed knots.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

2.1
and the standard fact that two connected
(1,1)
-tangles
T
and
T^{\prime}
are isotopic if and only if their closures are framed isotopic knots.
∎

3.
Renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of
\mathcal{C}

We henceforth restrict attention to the ribbon category
\mathcal{C}
of weight
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
-modules and write
F
for the Reshetikhin–Turaev functor
F_{\mathcal{C}}
.

3.1.
Ambidextrous modules

The idea of constructing a non-zero invariant from a knot by cutting to obtain a
(1,1)
-tangle does not immediately extend to links, as the following example shows.

Example 3.1
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
and
L
the Hopf link with components colored by
V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\beta}
. Up to isotopy, there are two choices of how to cut
L
. Cutting the strand colored by
V_{\alpha}
gives

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.76968pt}{-1.98611pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.3658pt}{-23.15257pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)=\begin{cases}q^{\beta\alpha}\frac{\{\alpha r\}}{\{\alpha\}}\cdot\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}}&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus r\mathbb{Z},\\
q^{\beta rz}\cdot(-1)^{(r+1)z}r\cdot\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}}&\mbox{if }\alpha=rz\in r\mathbb{Z}.\end{cases}

Indeed, the map defined by the above tangle is the composition

V_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}_{V_{\beta}}}V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}\otimes V_{\beta}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{c_{V_{\alpha},V_{\beta}}}V_{\beta}\otimes V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{c_{V_{\beta},V_{\alpha}}}V_{\alpha}\otimes V_{\beta}\otimes V_{\beta}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V_{\beta}}}V_{\alpha}.

As in the proof of
Theorem

1.19
, it suffices to compute the image under this map of a highest weight vector
v_{0}\in V_{\alpha}
. Let
\{w_{i}\mid 0\leq i\leq r-1\}
be a weight basis of
V_{\beta}
with dual basis
\{w_{i}^{\vee}\mid 0\leq i\leq r-1\}
. Then we have under the above composition

v_{0}\xmapsto{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{V_{\beta}}}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}v_{0}\otimes w_{i}\otimes w_{i}^{\vee}\xmapsto{c_{V_{\alpha},V_{\beta}}\otimes\mathrm{id}}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\beta+r-1-2i)/2}w_{i}\otimes v_{0}\otimes w_{i}^{\vee}\xmapsto{c_{V_{\beta},V_{\alpha}}\otimes\mathrm{id}}\\
\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\beta+r-1-2i)}v_{0}\otimes w_{i}\otimes w_{i}^{\vee}+\cdots\xmapsto{\mathrm{id}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V_{\beta}}}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\beta+r-1-2i)}q^{(\beta+r-1-2i)(1-r)}v_{0},

where the omitted quantity
\cdots
is a linear combination of terms of the form
E^{j}v_{0}\otimes w_{i+j}\otimes w_{i}^{\vee}
,
j>0
, and so is in the kernel of
\mathrm{id}_{V_{\alpha}}\otimes\widehat{\mathrm{ev}}_{V_{\beta}}
. We have

\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{(\alpha+r-1)(\beta+r-1-2i)}q^{(\beta+r-1-2i)(1-r)}=q^{\alpha(\beta+r-1)}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{-2\alpha i}.

If
\alpha\notin r\mathbb{Z}
, then
q^{-2\alpha}\neq 1
and the previous line evaluates to

q^{\alpha(\beta+r-1)}\frac{1-q^{-2\alpha r}}{1-q^{-2\alpha}}=q^{\alpha\beta}\frac{q^{\alpha r}-q^{-\alpha r}}{q^{\alpha}-q^{-\alpha}}=q^{\alpha\beta}\frac{\{\alpha r\}}{\{\alpha\}}.

If instead
\alpha=rz\in r\mathbb{Z}
, then

q^{rz(\beta+r-1)}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{-2rzi}=q^{rz(\beta+r-1)}r=(-1)^{rz+z}q^{\beta rz}r.

In particular, taking
r=2
with
\alpha=0
and
\beta=2
, we obtain

Equation

, we want to attach to the Hopf link colored by

and

a scalar given by cutting the Hopf link open to a

-tangle. However, we see that the scalar depends non-trivially on which strand we choose to cut.
∎

The following notion is the key to resolving the cutting ambiguity illustrated by the previous example.

, §3]
)
.

Lemma 3.3
.

is simple with non-vanishing quantum dimension, then

is ambidextrous.

Since
L
is obtained by taking the right and left partial traces of the
(1,1)
-tangles appearing in
Equation

10
, we find that both sides of this equation are equal to
\frac{\langle F(L)\rangle}{\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(V)}\mathrm{id}_{V}
.
∎

When
V
has vanishing quantum dimension, we need to investigate further.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
V,W\in\mathcal{C}
be simple objects such that
V\otimes W
is semisimple and multiplicity free. Then the algebra
\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V\otimes W)
is isomorphic to a direct sum of copies of
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Let
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
be pairwise non-isomorphic simples such that
V\otimes W\simeq U_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus U_{n}
. Schur’s Lemma implies algebra isomorphisms
\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V\otimes W)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(U_{i})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
. Then
V_{\eta}\otimes V_{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}
is semisimple and multiplicity free.

Proof.

Since
2\eta\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the object
V_{\eta}\otimes V_{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}_{\overline{2\eta}}
is semisimple by
Theorem

1.13
.
Hence, there exist unique integers
m_{2\eta_{r}-r+2i+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that

V_{\eta}\otimes V_{\eta}\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{r-1}V_{2\eta-r+2i+1}^{\oplus m_{2\eta_{r}-r+2i+1}}.

Consider
\mathbb{Q}[\mathbb{C}]
, the group algebra of
\mathbb{C}
, with basis
\{x^{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}}
. The
character
of
V\in\mathcal{C}
is
\text{ch}(V)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(V[\lambda])x^{\lambda}
. The explicit description of
V_{\alpha}
gives
\text{ch}(V_{\alpha})=x^{\alpha}[r]_{x}
, where
[r]_{x}=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{r-1-2i}
. We claim that the set

\mathcal{S}=\{\text{ch}(V_{2\eta-r+2i+1})\mid 0\leq i\leq r-1\}\subset\mathbb{Q}[\mathbb{C}]

is linearly independent. Suppose that
\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}a_{i}\text{ch}(V_{2\eta-r+2i+1})=0
for some
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Since all powers of
x
which appear in this equation lie on the same affine real line in
\mathbb{C}
, they are naturally ordered. The largest such power is
x^{2\eta+2(r-1)}
with coefficient
a_{r-1}
, resulting from
\text{ch}(V_{2\eta-r+2(r-1)+1})
. Hence,
a_{r-1}=0
. Continuing in this way shows that
a_{r-2}=\cdots=a_{0}=0
and
\mathcal{S}
is linearly independent.

The character of
V_{\eta}\otimes V_{\eta}
is
\text{ch}(V_{\eta})^{2}=x^{2\eta}[r]_{x}^{2}
. On the other hand,

\text{ch}(V_{\eta})^{2}=\big(\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}m_{2\eta-r+2i+1}x^{2\eta-r+2i}\big)[r]_{x}.

Setting each
m_{2\eta-r+1+2i}=1
, the right-hand side of the previous equation becomes

\left(\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}x^{2\eta-r+2i}\right)[r]_{x}=x^{2\eta}[r]^{2}_{x}=\text{ch}(V_{\eta})^{2}.

In view of the linear independence of
\mathcal{S}
, this completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
. Then
V_{\eta}
is ambidextrous.

Proof.

By
Lemmas

3.4
and
3.5
, the algebra
\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\eta}\otimes V_{\eta})
is commutative. Let
T
be a
(2,2)
-tangle whose open strands are colored by
V_{\eta}
. Then we have the following sequence of equalities, where we implicitly apply
F
to each tangle and the coupons are colored by
T
:

The second equality is implied by the commutativity of
\textnormal{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\eta}\otimes V_{\eta})
. The fifth and seventh equalities are each a combination of framed Reidemeister moves
RII
and
RIII
. The sixth equality holds by a combination of framed Reidemeister moves that depends on
T
. The other equalities hold by the indicated framed Reidemeister moves.
∎

3.2.
Modified quantum dimensions

Proposition 3.7
.

The equality

S^{\prime}(\beta,\alpha)=\begin{cases}q^{\beta\alpha}\frac{\{\alpha r\}}{\{\alpha\}}&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus r\mathbb{Z},\\
q^{\beta rz}\cdot(-1)^{(r+1)z}r&\mbox{if }\alpha=rz\in r\mathbb{Z}\end{cases}

holds. In particular,
S^{\prime}(\beta,\alpha)
is nonzero for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

This was computed in
3.1
.
∎

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
. The
modified quantum dimension with respect to
\eta
is the function
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}:\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)=\frac{S^{\prime}(\alpha,\eta)}{S^{\prime}(\eta,\alpha)}
.
∎

By
Proposition

3.7
, the modified quantum dimension
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}
is nowhere zero. Modified quantum dimensions associated to different parameters
\eta
are related as follows.

Proposition 3.9
.

For
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, the following equality holds:

\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)=\frac{\sin(\pi\frac{\eta}{r})\sin(\eta^{\prime}\pi)}{\sin(\eta\pi)\sin(\pi\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{r})}\mathbf{d}_{\eta^{\prime}}(\alpha).

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Proposition

3.7
and the definition of
\mathbf{d}_{(-)}
.
∎

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\right\rangle\;S^{\prime}(\eta,\beta).

By
Proposition

3.7
,
S^{\prime}(\eta,\alpha)
and
S^{\prime}(\eta,\beta)
are non-zero. We can therefore divide both sides of
Equation

11
by
S^{\prime}(\eta,\alpha)S^{\prime}(\eta,\beta)
to complete the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

For each
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, the module
V_{\alpha}
is ambidextrous. In particular, any simple module in
\mathcal{C}
is ambidextrous.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
. By
Theorem

3.6
, the module
V_{\eta}
is ambidextrous. By
Proposition

3.7
, the scalar
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)
is non-zero. The first statement now follows from taking
\alpha=\beta
in
Theorem

3.10
. Using the classification of simple objects of
\mathcal{C}
given in
Proposition

1.7
, the second claim follows from the above and
Lemma

3.3
.
∎

3.3.
Renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links

Denote by
\mathfrak{L}
the set of all framed colored links for which at least one of its colors is of the form
V_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
. We view
\mathfrak{L}
as a subset of morphisms of
\mathbf{Rib}_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Theorem 3.12

(
[
GPMT09
, Theorem 3]
)
.

Let
\eta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
. Then the map
F_{\eta}^{\prime}:\mathfrak{L}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by

F_{\eta}^{\prime}(L)=\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)\langle F(T)\rangle,

where
T
is a
(1,1)
-tangle whose closure is
L
and whose open strand is colored by
V_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, is a well-defined isotopy invariant of links in
\mathfrak{L}
.

Proof.

Well-definedness of
F_{\eta}^{\prime}
is the statement that
F_{\eta}^{\prime}(L)
is independent of the choice of
(1,1)
-tangle
T
used in its definition. If
T
and
T^{\prime}
are
(1,1)
-tangles constructed from
L
by cutting along strands colored by
V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\beta}
, respectively, then, in view of
Corollary

3.11
,
Theorem

3.10
shows that
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)\langle F(T)\rangle=\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\beta)\langle F(T^{\prime})\rangle
. Isotopy invariance of
F_{\eta}^{\prime}
follows from
Theorem

2.1
.
∎

Comparing the definition of
F_{\eta}^{\prime}(L)
with
Equation

9
shows that
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)
plays the role of
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(V_{\alpha})
in the standard theory. This justifies the term
modified quantum dimension
for the function
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}
.

3.4.
Basic properties and examples

We begin by discussing the dependence of the renormalized invariants of
Theorem

3.12
on the parameter
\eta
. By
Proposition

3.9
, the modified quantum dimensions functions associated to two such parameters
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
differ by an explicit global scalar. In fact, more is true.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
D:\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a function such that the assignment
L\mapsto D(\alpha)\langle F(T)\rangle
, where
T
is any
(1,1)
-tangle whose closure is isotopic to
L
and whose open strand is colored by
V_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
, is a well-defined invariant of colored framed links in
\mathfrak{L}
. Then, for any
\eta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
, there exists a scalar
d\in\mathbb{C}
such that
D=d\cdot\mathbf{d}_{\eta}
.

Proof.

Let
L
be a Hopf link with strands colored by
V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\eta}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
. The assumption of well-definedness of the invariant implies that
D(\alpha)S^{\prime}(\eta,\alpha)=D(\eta)S^{\prime}(\alpha,\eta)
, whence

D(\alpha)=D(\eta)\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)
. Taking
d=D(\eta)
proves the lemma.
∎

It follows from
Lemma

3.13
that renormalized invariants arising from a modified quantum dimension
\mathbf{d}_{\eta}
are effectively the only invariants that incorporate the cutting process described above. When
r=2
,
Lemma

3.13
can be strengthened. By
Proposition

3.9
, any function
D
that gives an invariant incorporating the cutting procedure is equal to
\pm\mathbf{d}_{\eta}
for some
\eta\in\mathbb{C}
, that is, the scalar
d
in
Lemma

3.13
can be taken to be
\pm 1
.

Remark 3.14
.

By
2.3
, the simple modules
S^{lr}_{n}
have non-zero quantum dimension and so, by
Lemma

3.3
, are ambidextrous. In view of this, there is a modification of
Theorem

3.12
in which the standard Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant is renormalized with respect to
S^{lr}_{n}
instead of
V_{\eta}
. The resulting invariant
F^{\prime}_{n,lr}
is defined on
\mathfrak{L}
as well as those knots
\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}
colored by at least one simple module of the form
S_{m}^{kr}
. A calculation as in
3.1
shows that the
S^{lr}_{n}
-renormalized quantum dimension
\mathbf{d}_{n}^{lr}
vanishes on
V_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,
F^{\prime}_{n,lr}
is zero on
\mathfrak{L}
. On the other hand, a direct calculation gives

\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(S^{kr}_{m})=\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(S^{lr}_{n})\cdot\mathbf{d}_{n}^{lr}(S^{kr}_{m}).

It follows that the restriction of
F^{\prime}_{n,lr}
to
\tilde{\mathfrak{L}}
is equal to
\textnormal{qdim}_{\mathcal{C}}(S^{lr}_{n})^{-1}
times the standard Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant
F
. From this perspective, the renormalized theory recovers the standard theory.
∎

Next, we describe the behavior of renormalized invariants under connect sum.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
, each of which have at least one strand colored by
V_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{Z}\cup r\mathbb{Z}
. Then the
\eta
-renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant of the connect sum
L\#L^{\prime}
along strands colored by
V_{\alpha}
satisfies

\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)F^{\prime}_{\eta}(L\#L^{\prime})=F^{\prime}_{\eta}(L)\cdot F^{\prime}_{\eta}(L^{\prime}).

Proof.

Consider a knot diagram for
L\#L^{\prime}
of the form

U
U^{\prime}
\alpha
\alpha

where the obvious closures of the
(2,0)
-tangle
U
and
(0,2)
-tangle
U^{\prime}
are knot diagrams for
L
and
L^{\prime}
, respectively, and we have written
\alpha
for the color
V_{\alpha}
. Cutting this diagram along one of the connecting strands gives

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\right\rangle=\mathbf{d}_{\eta}(\alpha)^{-1}F^{\prime}_{\eta}(L)F^{\prime}_{\eta}(L^{\prime}).

This gives the desired expression for
F^{\prime}_{\eta}(L\#L^{\prime})
.
∎

Example 3.16
.

Let
r=2
. Set
V=V_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
. Consider
V\otimes V
with basis
\{v_{0}\otimes v_{0},v_{0}\otimes v_{1},v_{1}\otimes v_{0},v_{1}\otimes v_{1}\}
. A direct computation gives

c_{V,V}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}q^{\frac{(\alpha+1)^{2}}{2}}&0&0&0\\
0&0&q^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(\alpha-1)}{2}}&0\\
0&q^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(\alpha-1)}{2}}&q^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(\alpha-1)}{2}}\{1-\alpha\}&0\\
0&0&0&q^{\frac{(\alpha-1)^{2}}{2}}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

For example, the
(3,2)
and
(3,3)
entries of
c_{V,V}
are the coefficients of
c_{V,V}(v_{1}\otimes v_{0})
:

\displaystyle c_{V,V}(v_{1}\otimes v_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(q^{H\otimes H/2}(1+\frac{\{1\}^{2}}{\{1\}}E\otimes F)(v_{1}\otimes v_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau(q^{H\otimes H/2}(v_{1}\otimes v_{0}+\frac{\{1\}^{2}}{\{1\}}Ev_{1}\otimes Fv_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{(\alpha-1)(\alpha+1)}{2}}v_{0}\otimes v_{1}+q^{\frac{(\alpha-1)(\alpha+1)}{2}}\{1-\alpha\}v_{1}\otimes v_{0}.

Using the explicit formula for
c_{V,V}
, we verify the equality

(12)

q^{-\frac{(\alpha+1)(\alpha-1)}{2}}c_{V,V}-q^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(\alpha-1)}{2}}c_{V,V}^{-1}=\{\alpha+1\}\mathrm{id}_{V\otimes V}.

Recall from the proof of
Theorem

1.19
that
\theta_{V}=q^{-\frac{(\alpha+1)(\alpha-1)}{2}}\mathrm{id}_{V}
. Define
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(L)=q^{\frac{(\alpha+1)(\alpha-1)}{2}{\operatorname{wr}}(L)}F^{\prime}(L)
, where
{\operatorname{wr}}(L)
is the writhe of
L
. Then
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is an invariant of oriented links colored by
V_{\alpha}
. This is an instance of the deframing procedure, explained, for example, in
[
JM19
, §3.3]
. The relation (
12
) implies that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
satisfies the Alexander skein relation with
t=q^{\alpha+1}
.
∎

We end this section with some calculations of renormalized invariants for knots with few crossings.

Example 3.17
.

Using the result of
2.5
, the renormalized invariant of the right-handed trefoil
K=3_{1}
colored by a simple module
V_{\alpha}
is

F^{\prime}_{\eta}(K)=\frac{\{\eta r\}\{\alpha\}}{\{\eta\}\{\alpha r\}}q^{\frac{3}{2}(\alpha+r-1)^{2}+(\alpha+r-1)(1-r)}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}q^{i(-3\alpha-r+i)}\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\{i-j-\alpha\}.\qed

Example 3.18
.

Proceeding analogously to
3.17
, the renormalized invariant of the figure eight knot
K=4_{1}
colored by a simple module
V_{\alpha}
is

F^{\prime}_{\eta}(K)=\frac{\{\eta r\}\{\alpha\}}{\{\eta\}\{\alpha r\}}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{k=0}^{r-i}q^{(-\alpha+r-1-2i)(\alpha-r+1)/2}q^{-(-\alpha+r-1-2(i-j))(i+r-1-j)}\cdot\\
q^{-(-\alpha+r-1-2(i-j+k))(\alpha+r-1-2(i+k))/2}q^{j(j-1)/2}q^{k(k-1)/2}q^{(i+k-r+1)(i+k-r)/2}\cdot\\
\prod_{x=r-1}^{r-1-j}\frac{\{x\}\{x-\alpha\}}{\{j\}!}\prod_{y=i-j}^{i-j+k}\frac{\{y\}\{y+\alpha\}}{\{k\}!})\prod_{z=i+k}^{r-1}\frac{\{z\}\{z-\alpha\}}{\{l\}!}.\qed

4.
Further reading

In this final section, we give a brief sample of recent developments in renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev theory.

The construction of renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links, given in
Theorem

3.10
for the category of weight modules over
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, applies more generally to certain non-semisimple ribbon categories
[
GPMT09
]
. The key new notions in this more general context are
modified traces
(and so modified quantum dimensions) and ambidextrous objects
[
GKPM11
,
GKPM13
,
GKPM22
,
GPM18
,
FOG18
,
GR20
,
BBG21
]
. Renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links have been studied for categories of weight modules over unrolled quantum groups of complex simple Lie algebras
[
GPM13
]
and Lie superalgebras
[
GPM07
,
GPM10
]
. The renormalized invariants are motivated by and recover previous
ad hoc
renormalized invariants
[
KS91
,
ADO92
,
Vir06
,
GPM10
]
.

The extension of the renormalized theory from links to
3
-manifolds was achieved in
[
CGPM14
]
for the category of weight modules over
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, where properties
(P2)
and
(P3)
are serious obstructions. In general, the additional data and constraints on the input ribbon category required to obtain a
3
-manifold invariant are termed
non-degenerate relative pre-modularity
. The resulting
3
-manifold invariants have novel properties, including the ability to distinguish homotopy classes of lens spaces and connections with the Volume Conjecture, and are related to earlier non-semisimple
3
-manifold invariants, including those of Hennings
[
Hen96
]
and Kerler–Lyubashenko
[
KL01
]
. See
[
DRGPM18
,
DRGG+23
]
. Further examples of
3
-manifold invariants associated to non-degenerate relative pre-modular categories are studied in
[
AGPM21
,
BH21
,
Ha18
,
BI22
]
. For connections between standard and renormalized Reshetikhin–Turaev invariants of links and 3-manifolds, see
[
CGPM15a
,
CGP21
,
DRGPM20
,
Mor22
]
.

The further extension of renormalized
3
-manifold invariants to three dimensional topological quantum field theories (TQFTs) was first accomplished in the case of weight modules over
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))

[
BCGPM16
]
. The TQFTs have interesting features, including the extension of Reidemeister torsion to a TQFT and the possibility of producing representations of mapping class groups that are faithful modulo their centers. A general framework for the construction of (extended) TQFTs from
relative modular categories
was given by De Renzi
[
DR22
]
, generalizing the so-called universal construction of semisimple TQFTs
[
BHMV95
]
. See
[
BDR21
]
for an overview of this circle of ideas. Further examples of TQFTs from renormalized invariants are constructed and studied in
[
DRGG+22
,
GY22
]
.

At present, the main source of relative modular categories is the representation theory of unrolled quantum groups, thereby making this class of quantum groups central to non-semisimple topology. Motivated by the success of rational conformal field theoretic techniques in semisimple topology, a number of authors have pursued a conjectural logarithmic variant of the Kazhdan–Lusztig correspondence, which asserts an equivalence between categories of weight modules over unrolled quantum groups and modules over non-rational, or logarithmic, vertex operator algebras. Much progress has been made in the case of
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
, where connections between weight modules and the singlet, triplet and Feigin–Tipunin algebras have been found
[
CMR18
,
CR22
]
.

Finally, there has been exciting progress in connecting non-semisimple mathematical TQFTs to physical quantum field theories. This can be seen as a non-semisimple generalization of the celebrated connection between compact Chern–Simons theory and Reshetikhin–Turaev TQFTs
[
Wit89
,
RT91
]
. The case of TQFTs arising from
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))
is studied in
[
CDGG21
]
, where it is connected to a topological twist of
3
d
\mathcal{N}=4
Chern–Simons matter theory with gauge group
SU(2)
. Similarly, TQFTs arising from an unrolled quantization of the Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{gl}(1|1)
were shown in
[
GY22
]
to be related to supergroup Chern–Simons theories with gauge group
\mathfrak{psl}(1|1)
and
U(1|1)
. A key feature in both physical realizations is the presence of global symmetry groups, allowing the quantum field theories to be coupled to background flat connections. Further physical studies of such quantum field theories can be found in
[
GHN+21
,
Jag22
]
. In condensed matter physics, Levin and Wen used unitary spherical fusion categories to give a mathematical foundation of topological order and string-net condensation
[
LW05
]
. Recently, this construction was extended to the setting of the non-semisimple category of weight modules over
\overline{U}^{H}_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))

[
GLPMS22a
,
GLPMS22b
]
.
